I just feel like if I look soft, people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. So, honestly, it just doesn't feel like some medical emergency to me. I don't really see what they're seeing, though. From my side, I just notice that my suits fit a lot sharper now. But my weight has kept dropping since my divorce, so I guess that's why they pushed me to come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a healthy weight right now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I am. I mean, I know everyone is making a big deal about my weight dropping, but... I still jog every day. I'm out there working my territory, and I'm hitting all my sales numbers. So, honestly, it just doesn't feel like some medical emergency to me. I'm functioning just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently lower than it should be?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's lower than it should be. Like I said, I feel fine. I'm just leaner now, which I think is a good thing. I really don't think I'm underweight or anything.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, honestly, I am. If I step on the scale and it's even a pound higher—like after a big client dinner or a weekend out in Miami—I get this... this panic. It just feels like I'm losing control. And right away, I start planning exactly how I'm going to cut back the next day to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight influence what or how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely does. Most days, I just keep it to coffee in the morning. Maybe I'll grab a protein bar while I'm driving between sales calls. And then at night, I'll have something small, like some grilled chicken, but no rice or anything heavy. It's just... I feel like once I sit down and actually eat a full meal, it just feels like I completely messed up the whole day. So, yeah, I keep it pretty tight to avoid that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about how the food you eat will affect your body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... well, when I look in the mirror, especially before a client meeting or a date, I zero right in on my stomach and my face. If I think I look soft, I just feel like people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. So, yeah, I definitely connect what I'm eating to how my shape looks in those moments.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do that. I've never made myself throw up. I just... I handle it by controlling what I eat in the first place, like I mentioned. So, no, I don't do the vomiting thing.\n\nDoctor: How much weight have you lost since your divorce?\n\nPatient: I don't have an exact number of pounds, but my weight has just kept dropping since the divorce. Honestly, the main reason I'm even here talking to you is because HR at work and my sister keep getting on my case, saying I've gotten way too thin. But from my side of things... I just see that my suits fit a lot sharper now.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being underweight?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I know everyone is making a huge deal about my weight right now, but when I look in the mirror, I just don't see a problem. I still jog, I'm out working my territory, and I'm hitting my sales numbers. So... it just doesn't feel like some medical emergency to me.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as you really are?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think I see what's there. But... I guess when I look in the mirror, especially right before a client meeting or a date, I tend to zero right in on my stomach and my face. If I think I look soft in those areas, it really bothers me. I just feel like if I look soft, people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. And in sales, you really can't afford to give off that impression.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your weight is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: I guess it must be. I mean, my sister and the people at work are obviously seeing something that makes them think I'm too thin, but I just don't see it that way. I look at myself and I just see someone who's staying in shape and keeping a sharp edge for work. So yeah, there's definitely a disconnect between what they're seeing and what I'm seeing.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as fat even when others say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I see myself as fat, exactly. It's more that I just see areas where I could be tighter, you know? Like I said, I focus on my stomach and my face. I don't look in the mirror and think I'm massively overweight, I just see parts of me that look a little soft, even if other people are telling me I'm too skinny. I just don't see the \"too skinny\" part.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would know if you were underweight, even if you didn't use a scale?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean... given that I don't see what my coworkers or my sister see, I probably wouldn't know. If I didn't have a scale telling me a number, I'd just be going off the mirror. And like I said, when I look in the mirror, I'm really just looking for those soft spots. So... no, I guess I wouldn't really be able to tell on my own.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained a small amount of weight, even if it meant you were at a healthy weight for your height?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely would be. Honestly, if the scale is even a pound higher after, say, a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this... this panic, like I'm losing control. It doesn't really matter to me if it's supposed to be a healthy weight. If I see that number go up, I just immediately start planning how to cut back the next day to get it back down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's... well, when I look in the mirror, especially before a client meeting or a date, I zero right in on my stomach and my face. If I think I look soft, I just feel like people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. So, yeah, I definitely connect what I'm eating to how my shape looks in those moments.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do that. I've never made myself throw up. I just... I handle it by controlling what I eat in the first place, like I mentioned. So, no, I don't do the vomiting thing.\n\nDoctor: How much weight have you lost since your divorce?\n\nPatient: I don't have an exact number of pounds, but my weight has just kept dropping since the divorce. Honestly, the main reason I'm even here talking to you is because HR at work and my sister keep getting on my case, saying I've gotten way too thin. But from my side of things... I just see that my suits fit a lot sharper now.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being underweight?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I know everyone is making a huge deal about my weight right now, but when I look in the mirror, I just don't see a problem. I still jog, I'm out working my territory, and I'm hitting my sales numbers. So... it just doesn't feel like some medical emergency to me.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as you really are?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think I see what's there. But... I guess when I look in the mirror, especially right before a client meeting or a date, I tend to zero right in on my stomach and my face. If I think I look soft in those areas, it really bothers me. I just feel like if I look soft, people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. And in sales, you really can't afford to give off that impression.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your weight is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: I guess it must be. I mean, my sister and the people at work are obviously seeing something that makes them think I'm too thin, but I just don't see it that way. I look at myself and I just see someone who's staying in shape and keeping a sharp edge for work. So yeah, there's definitely a disconnect between what they're seeing and what I'm seeing.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as fat even when others say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I see myself as fat, exactly. It's more that I just see areas where I could be tighter, you know? Like I said, I focus on my stomach and my face. I don't look in the mirror and think I'm massively overweight, I just see parts of me that look a little soft, even if other people are telling me I'm too skinny. I just don't see the \"too skinny\" part.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would know if you were underweight, even if you didn't use a scale?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean... given that I don't see what my coworkers or my sister see, I probably wouldn't know. If I didn't have a scale telling me a number, I'd just be going off the mirror. And like I said, when I look in the mirror, I'm really just looking for those soft spots. So... no, I guess I wouldn't really be able to tell on my own.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained a small amount of weight, even if it meant you were at a healthy weight for your height?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely would be. Honestly, if the scale is even a pound higher after, say, a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this... this panic, like I'm losing control. It doesn't really matter to me if it's supposed to be a healthy weight. If I see that number go up, I just immediately start planning how to cut back the next day to get it back down.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being the same weight as others your height and build?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I don't tend to compare myself to other guys my height. I'm just looking at myself and those specific areas I mentioned. I couldn't really tell you if I look the same weight as someone else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I just see that my suits fit a lot sharper now.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being underweight?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I know everyone is making a huge deal about my weight right now, but when I look in the mirror, I just don't see a problem. I still jog, I'm out working my territory, and I'm hitting my sales numbers. So... it just doesn't feel like some medical emergency to me.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as you really are?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think I see what's there. But... I guess when I look in the mirror, especially right before a client meeting or a date, I tend to zero right in on my stomach and my face. If I think I look soft in those areas, it really bothers me. I just feel like if I look soft, people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. And in sales, you really can't afford to give off that impression.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your weight is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: I guess it must be. I mean, my sister and the people at work are obviously seeing something that makes them think I'm too thin, but I just don't see it that way. I look at myself and I just see someone who's staying in shape and keeping a sharp edge for work. So yeah, there's definitely a disconnect between what they're seeing and what I'm seeing.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as fat even when others say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I see myself as fat, exactly. It's more that I just see areas where I could be tighter, you know? Like I said, I focus on my stomach and my face. I don't look in the mirror and think I'm massively overweight, I just see parts of me that look a little soft, even if other people are telling me I'm too skinny. I just don't see the \"too skinny\" part.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would know if you were underweight, even if you didn't use a scale?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean... given that I don't see what my coworkers or my sister see, I probably wouldn't know. If I didn't have a scale telling me a number, I'd just be going off the mirror. And like I said, when I look in the mirror, I'm really just looking for those soft spots. So... no, I guess I wouldn't really be able to tell on my own.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained a small amount of weight, even if it meant you were at a healthy weight for your height?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely would be. Honestly, if the scale is even a pound higher after, say, a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this... this panic, like I'm losing control. It doesn't really matter to me if it's supposed to be a healthy weight. If I see that number go up, I just immediately start planning how to cut back the next day to get it back down.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being the same weight as others your height and build?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I don't tend to compare myself to other guys my height. I'm just looking at myself and those specific areas I mentioned. I couldn't really tell you if I look the same weight as someone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a possibility that your perception of your own body shape or weight might be inaccurate?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess it's possible. Since everyone else keeps telling me I'm too thin, and I don't see it that way, there's a chance I'm not seeing things exactly right. But it's hard to really believe that when I'm the one looking at my own body every day. So, I don't know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I think so, yeah. It definitely feels like the most important thing. When I look in the mirror, especially before a big sales meeting or, you know, going on a date, I immediately zero in on my stomach and my face. If I think I look even a little bit soft in those areas, it completely throws me off. I just feel like people are going to see me as sloppy, or... or weak. So, yeah, how I look and how much I weigh definitely dictates how I feel about myself on any given day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. So... no, I guess I wouldn't really be able to tell on my own.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained a small amount of weight, even if it meant you were at a healthy weight for your height?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely would be. Honestly, if the scale is even a pound higher after, say, a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this... this panic, like I'm losing control. It doesn't really matter to me if it's supposed to be a healthy weight. If I see that number go up, I just immediately start planning how to cut back the next day to get it back down.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being the same weight as others your height and build?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I don't tend to compare myself to other guys my height. I'm just looking at myself and those specific areas I mentioned. I couldn't really tell you if I look the same weight as someone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a possibility that your perception of your own body shape or weight might be inaccurate?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess it's possible. Since everyone else keeps telling me I'm too thin, and I don't see it that way, there's a chance I'm not seeing things exactly right. But it's hard to really believe that when I'm the one looking at my own body every day. So, I don't know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I think so, yeah. It definitely feels like the most important thing. When I look in the mirror, especially before a big sales meeting or, you know, going on a date, I immediately zero in on my stomach and my face. If I think I look even a little bit soft in those areas, it completely throws me off. I just feel like people are going to see me as sloppy, or... or weak. So, yeah, how I look and how much I weigh definitely dictates how I feel about myself on any given day.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight stop you from doing things you want to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, I still manage my sales route fine, but... I definitely avoid client dinners now. And dates, too. Unless I know exactly where we're going and I can control the menu, I just won't go. So lately, I've been turning down a lot of invitations and just keeping more to myself. It's just easier than dealing with the stress of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Honestly, I'm here because HR at my company and... well, my sister, they both keep saying I've gotten too thin. I don't really see what they're seeing, though. From my side, I just notice that my suits fit a lot sharper now. But my weight has kept dropping since my divorce, so I guess that's why they pushed me to come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a healthy weight right now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I am. I mean, I know everyone is making a big deal about my weight dropping, but... I still jog every day. I'm out there working my territory, and I'm hitting all my sales numbers. So, honestly, it just doesn't feel like some medical emergency to me. I'm functioning just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently lower than it should be?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's lower than it should be. Like I said, I feel fine. I'm just leaner now, which I think is a good thing. I really don't think I'm underweight or anything.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, honestly, I am. If I step on the scale and it's even a pound higher—like after a big client dinner or a weekend out in Miami—I get this... this panic. It just feels like I'm losing control. And right away, I start planning exactly how I'm going to cut back the next day to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight influence what or how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely does. Most days, I just keep it to coffee in the morning. Maybe I'll grab a protein bar while I'm driving between sales calls. And then at night, I'll have something small, like some grilled chicken, but no rice or anything heavy. It's just... I feel like once I sit down and actually eat a full meal, it just feels like I completely messed up the whole day. So, yeah, I keep it pretty tight to avoid that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about how the food you eat will affect your body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... well, when I look in the mirror, especially before a client meeting or a date, I zero right in on my stomach and my face. If I think I look soft, I just feel like people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. So, yeah, I definitely connect what I'm eating to how my shape looks in those moments.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do that. I've never made myself throw up. I just... I handle it by controlling what I eat in the first place, like I mentioned. So, no, I don't do the vomiting thing.\n\nDoctor: How much weight have you lost since your divorce?\n\nPatient: I don't have an exact number of pounds, but my weight has just kept dropping since the divorce. Honestly, the main reason I'm even here talking to you is because HR at work and my sister keep getting on my case, saying I've gotten way too thin. But from my side of things... I just see that my suits fit a lot sharper now.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being underweight?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I know everyone is making a huge deal about my weight right now, but when I look in the mirror, I just don't see a problem. I still jog, I'm out working my territory, and I'm hitting my sales numbers. So... it just doesn't feel like some medical emergency to me.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as you really are?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think I see what's there. But... I guess when I look in the mirror, especially right before a client meeting or a date, I tend to zero right in on my stomach and my face. If I think I look soft in those areas, it really bothers me. I just feel like if I look soft, people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. And in sales, you really can't afford to give off that impression.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your weight is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: I guess it must be. I mean, my sister and the people at work are obviously seeing something that makes them think I'm too thin, but I just don't see it that way. I look at myself and I just see someone who's staying in shape and keeping a sharp edge for work. So yeah, there's definitely a disconnect between what they're seeing and what I'm seeing.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as fat even when others say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I see myself as fat, exactly. It's more that I just see areas where I could be tighter, you know? Like I said, I focus on my stomach and my face. I don't look in the mirror and think I'm massively overweight, I just see parts of me that look a little soft, even if other people are telling me I'm too skinny. I just don't see the \"too skinny\" part.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would know if you were underweight, even if you didn't use a scale?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean... given that I don't see what my coworkers or my sister see, I probably wouldn't know. If I didn't have a scale telling me a number, I'd just be going off the mirror. And like I said, when I look in the mirror, I'm really just looking for those soft spots. So... no, I guess I wouldn't really be able to tell on my own.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained a small amount of weight, even if it meant you were at a healthy weight for your height?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely would be. But my weight has kept dropping since my divorce, so I guess that's why they pushed me to come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are at a healthy weight right now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I am. I mean, I know everyone is making a big deal about my weight dropping, but... I still jog every day. I'm out there working my territory, and I'm hitting all my sales numbers. So, honestly, it just doesn't feel like some medical emergency to me. I'm functioning just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently lower than it should be?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think it's lower than it should be. Like I said, I feel fine. I'm just leaner now, which I think is a good thing. I really don't think I'm underweight or anything.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, honestly, I am. If I step on the scale and it's even a pound higher—like after a big client dinner or a weekend out in Miami—I get this... this panic. It just feels like I'm losing control. And right away, I start planning exactly how I'm going to cut back the next day to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight influence what or how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely does. Most days, I just keep it to coffee in the morning. Maybe I'll grab a protein bar while I'm driving between sales calls. And then at night, I'll have something small, like some grilled chicken, but no rice or anything heavy. It's just... I feel like once I sit down and actually eat a full meal, it just feels like I completely messed up the whole day. So, yeah, I keep it pretty tight to avoid that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about how the food you eat will affect your body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... well, when I look in the mirror, especially before a client meeting or a date, I zero right in on my stomach and my face. If I think I look soft, I just feel like people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. So, yeah, I definitely connect what I'm eating to how my shape looks in those moments.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do that. I've never made myself throw up. I just... I handle it by controlling what I eat in the first place, like I mentioned. So, no, I don't do the vomiting thing.\n\nDoctor: How much weight have you lost since your divorce?\n\nPatient: I don't have an exact number of pounds, but my weight has just kept dropping since the divorce. Honestly, the main reason I'm even here talking to you is because HR at work and my sister keep getting on my case, saying I've gotten way too thin. But from my side of things... I just see that my suits fit a lot sharper now.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being underweight?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I know everyone is making a huge deal about my weight right now, but when I look in the mirror, I just don't see a problem. I still jog, I'm out working my territory, and I'm hitting my sales numbers. So... it just doesn't feel like some medical emergency to me.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as you really are?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think I see what's there. But... I guess when I look in the mirror, especially right before a client meeting or a date, I tend to zero right in on my stomach and my face. If I think I look soft in those areas, it really bothers me. I just feel like if I look soft, people are going to see me as sloppy or weak. And in sales, you really can't afford to give off that impression.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your weight is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: I guess it must be. I mean, my sister and the people at work are obviously seeing something that makes them think I'm too thin, but I just don't see it that way. I look at myself and I just see someone who's staying in shape and keeping a sharp edge for work. So yeah, there's definitely a disconnect between what they're seeing and what I'm seeing.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as fat even when others say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I see myself as fat, exactly. It's more that I just see areas where I could be tighter, you know? Like I said, I focus on my stomach and my face. I don't look in the mirror and think I'm massively overweight, I just see parts of me that look a little soft, even if other people are telling me I'm too skinny. I just don't see the \"too skinny\" part.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would know if you were underweight, even if you didn't use a scale?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean... given that I don't see what my coworkers or my sister see, I probably wouldn't know. If I didn't have a scale telling me a number, I'd just be going off the mirror. And like I said, when I look in the mirror, I'm really just looking for those soft spots. So... no, I guess I wouldn't really be able to tell on my own.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained a small amount of weight, even if it meant you were at a healthy weight for your height?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely would be. Honestly, if the scale is even a pound higher after, say, a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this... this panic, like I'm losing control. It doesn't really matter to me if it's supposed to be a healthy weight. If I see that number go up, I just immediately start planning how to cut back the next day to get it back down.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being the same weight as others your height and build?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I don't tend to compare myself to other guys my height. I'm just looking at myself and those specific areas I mentioned. I couldn't really tell you if I look the same weight as someone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a possibility that your perception of your own body shape or weight might be inaccurate?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess it's possible. Since everyone else keeps telling me I'm too thin, and I don't see it that way, there's a chance I'm not seeing things exactly right. But it's hard to really believe that when I'm the one looking at my own body every day. So, I don't know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I think so, yeah. So... no, I guess I wouldn't really be able to tell on my own.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained a small amount of weight, even if it meant you were at a healthy weight for your height?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely would be. Honestly, if the scale is even a pound higher after, say, a client dinner or a weekend out in Miami, I get this... this panic, like I'm losing control. It doesn't really matter to me if it's supposed to be a healthy weight. If I see that number go up, I just immediately start planning how to cut back the next day to get it back down.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being the same weight as others your height and build?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I don't tend to compare myself to other guys my height. I'm just looking at myself and those specific areas I mentioned. I couldn't really tell you if I look the same weight as someone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a possibility that your perception of your own body shape or weight might be inaccurate?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess it's possible. Since everyone else keeps telling me I'm too thin, and I don't see it that way, there's a chance I'm not seeing things exactly right. But it's hard to really believe that when I'm the one looking at my own body every day. So, I don't know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I think so, yeah. It definitely feels like the most important thing. When I look in the mirror, especially before a big sales meeting or, you know, going on a date, I immediately zero in on my stomach and my face. If I think I look even a little bit soft in those areas, it completely throws me off. I just feel like people are going to see me as sloppy, or... or weak. So, yeah, how I look and how much I weigh definitely dictates how I feel about myself on any given day.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight stop you from doing things you want to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, I still manage my sales route fine, but... I definitely avoid client dinners now. And dates, too. Unless I know exactly where we're going and I can control the menu, I just won't go. So lately, I've been turning down a lot of invitations and just keeping more to myself. It's just easier than dealing with the stress of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be worried about your weight if everyone around you told you that you look healthy and normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'm pretty sure I would still be worried. I mean, people already tell me I'm too thin, and that doesn't really change how I feel or what I see in the mirror. Even if everyone said I looked perfectly healthy and normal... I think I'd still be obsessing over my stomach and my face. It's more about how I feel about myself than what other people are saying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see yourself as needing to lose weight even if a doctor told you your weight is normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Honestly, I've had doctors tell me before that I need to put on a few pounds, and it didn't really change anything for me. If a doctor said my weight was perfectly normal... I'd probably just think they were comparing me to some average chart that doesn't apply to me. I'd still look in the mirror and see the areas I need to fix. So, yeah, a doctor's opinion wouldn't really stop me from wanting to lose more."
  }
